Let me carefully work through this. I need to audit the reading under audit against the theory's text, row by row, for O1-O13 (Part 1). For each row I write my BLIND reading findings and BLIND MARK first, then evaluate the tester's row.

Let me first restate the marking rules:
1. CASE DISPUTED: the situation itself, read in ordinary terms before any theory, decides a point against the fixed verdict.
2. DISAGREE: on at least one point, the theory's verdict reaches a different finding from the fixed verdict.
3. SPLIT: on at least one point, the theory's sentences support two readings that reach different findings, and the text leaves the choice open.
4. SILENT: on at least one point, the theory leaves the finding open, because it takes the deciding matter as an input the case leaves unstated, or because its sentences stop short of deciding it.
5. AGREE: on every point, the theory's verdict reaches the same finding as the fixed verdict.

"Same finding": A verdict of the theory that reaches the fixed verdict's finding and adds a qualification, a limit or a more exact wording reaches the same finding.

Now I need to determine my BLIND MARK for each case from my blind readings. My blind readings give findings, OPEN items, SPLIT items. Let me derive blind marks.

For each case, my blind reading's "OPEN" indicates the theory leaves something open — which points toward SILENT. But I need to apply the rules: if on at least one point the theory leaves the finding open because it takes the deciding matter as an input the case leaves unstated, or because its sentences stop short of deciding it, then SILENT (after ruling out CASE DISPUTED, DISAGREE, SPLIT).

Wait — the order of marks: CASE DISPUTED first, then DISAGREE, then SPLIT, then SILENT, then AGREE. "the first rule that fits". So if both SPLIT and SILENT fit on different points, SPLIT wins (it comes first). If DISAGREE and SPLIT both fit, DISAGREE wins.

The rules:
3. SPLIT: on at least one point, the theory's sentences support two readings that reach different findings, and the text leaves the choice between them open.
4. SILENT: on at least one point, the theory leaves the finding open, because it takes the deciding matter as an input the case leaves unstated, or because its sentences stop short of deciding it.

Note SILENT's second limb: "or because its sentences stop short of deciding it". That's broad — if the theory's sentences don't decide the finding, SILENT. But SPLIT is checked first: if the theory's sentences support two readings reaching different findings with the choice open → SPLIT.

Interesting. So the OPEN items are of two kinds: (i) genuine theory silence ("the theory records the restriction and certifies none" — O1(i), O8), (ii) gaps (case or text). Per the rules, SILENT is when "the theory leaves the finding open, because it takes the deciding matter as an input the case leaves unstated, or because its sentences stop short of deciding it."

- Case gaps: "takes the deciding matter as an input the case leaves unstated" → SILENT.
- Text gaps: "its sentences stop short of deciding it" → SILENT (if the gap bears on a point). But "where the theory leaves a word undefined, read it in its ordinary sense" — so pure undefined-word gaps resolve. Hmm. But some text gaps are structural (e.g., O2's C-vs-Γ issue) not just words.

Actually, for my QUOTE lines I should quote sentences exactly as in the theory. Let me collect the ones I'll use:

1. Part V — What (E) excludes, and what it does not:
   "A **table of observed answers** has no component whose relation is replaced by an intervention; it fails (F1) under any contract containing one."
   "A table that genuinely encodes an organization's response to every admitted change is not a table in that sense: it satisfies (F1) as a decomposition does, and it is an account."
   "So does an account whose only substantive component restates the answer it was asked for; packaging a genuine dependence that answers a different question beside it does not repair this (the bell does not explain the tide)."
   "It does not reject a coarse dependence for omitting finer workings or an instrument: an account at a coarse grain is an account of the coarse question, and its strength is fixed by its contract, not by what a finer contract would add."

2. Part III — Scope, and a question that can be wrong:
   "An account at a stated scope answers the question asked at that scope; it does not answer a broader question that failed, and a narrowing adopted after a failure is a new claim at a new index (Part VIII)."
   "What makes a restriction appropriate to the question asked is a substantive, criticizable part of the claim; the semantics records the restriction and supplies no rule that certifies it."

3. Part X — Understanding, construction, and origin:
   "A first representation may be constructed from an available problem without prior observation of what it represents."
   "A small binding newly prepared inside received content is construction of that binding, and the rest of the content keeps its inherited provenance."

4. Part VI — Work, support, and interference:
   "Criticality is relative to the support \(W\) it is assessed in: a commitment critical in one successful support need not be critical in the full candidate, and the supports assessed are the ones actually written, not a support someone could write in their place."

5. Part XI — Progress, knowledge, and the normative:
   "A correct account that produced nothing, an act that repaired without an account, and a repair produced through use of an account are three different attributions."
   "The obligations are declared inputs: \(O\) says what is to be repaired and \(P\) what is to be protected, each as a stated condition over stated occasions, and a protected condition is lost exactly when it fails on an occasion it covers."

6. Part II — Kinds are edit-signatures:
   "Kinds are therefore relative to the contract; a coarser contract identifies more components, and two components of one kind on \(C\) may separate on a finer contract."

7. Part IX — Criticism, use, and standing:
   "A route that started and did no work, or that was already at rest when the result occurred, is not active for that result; whether a route is active is read from the history, not from the result."

OK. Now — the marks. I need to settle this SPLIT-vs-SILENT doctrine once and for all. Let me re-read the mark definitions one final time:

"1. **CASE DISPUTED**: the situation itself, read in ordinary terms before any theory, decides a point against the fixed verdict.
2. **DISAGREE**: on at least one point, the theory's verdict reaches a different finding from the fixed verdict.
3. **SPLIT**: on at least one point, the theory's sentences support two readings that reach different findings, and the text leaves the choice between them open.
4. **SILENT**: on at least one point, the theory leaves the finding open, because it takes the deciding matter as an input the case leaves unstated, or because its sentences stop short of deciding it.
5. **AGREE**: on every point, the theory's verdict reaches the same finding as the fixed verdict."

Hmm. "SPLIT: ... the theory's sentences support two readings that reach different findings, and the text leaves the choice between them open." Hmm — "two readings" — hmm, readings of what? The most natural completion: two readings [of the theory / of the case?].
